          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods on Mistral-7B FOLIO and adds SC from three model families. SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling. Debate breaks through to $\\kappa = 0.15$ ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 4.44$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three model families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming the ceiling is architecture-independent (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}). This constitutes a Pareto frontier (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}): methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. Cross-model SICA (Mistral+LLaMA+Qwen, $K{=}12$) provides direct evidence: mixing three families reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yet yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$)---even cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within the self-extraction paradigm. The only demonstrated path past this frontier requires signal from outside the model's distribution, consistent with the oracle result (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) and published systems that bypass self-extraction \\citep{xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, pan2023logiclm}.",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Test-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.
4	The dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.
5	SC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.
6	\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.
7	The gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.
8	
9	Confidence-weighted \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.
10	Recent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.
11	A simpler question precedes these: can the \emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?
12	Self-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.
13	Yet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.
14	
15	We ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \citep{austensmith1996}.
16	When independence fails \citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.
17	Measuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
18	The bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.
19	Chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
20	When the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
21	Self-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.
22	
23	We study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3} (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
24	We evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 instances with four-seed replication (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}).
25	More than twenty remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale tested.
26	
27	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL yields $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
28	The central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
29	Whether larger models or trained extractors can overcome this remains open.
30	
31	Our contributions are:
32	\begin{enumerate}
33	\item \textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off.} Fleiss' $\kappa$ generalization across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a Pareto frontier: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$). More than twenty remediation strategies all fail (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
34	
35	\item \textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} Self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer: bias ratio $4.97\times$ on incorrect problems, 94\% error overlap with SC. The causal chain---model generates answer, extracts consistent FOL, Z3 aggregation mirrors majority vote---explains why self-extraction cannot correct errors the model has already committed (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
36	
37	\item \textbf{Process-Level Independence Quantification.} Chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.41$), establishing that reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answer distributions suggest. Combined with Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across 15 conditions and three model families, this quantifies the independence ceiling for same-model trace aggregation (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}, \S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
38	
39	\item \textbf{Regime-Dependent Pattern and Diagnostics.} Three failure modes---confirmation bias (SC $\geq 60\%$), non-formalizability, error amplification (SC $\leq 40\%$)---create regime boundaries. $C_0$ (multi-candidate rate) combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\S\ref{sec:main-results}).
40	\end{enumerate}
```

_source line 9771 · 2026-05-20T11:31:18.285Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KB9As5W8zb5iEtKFsSfSaS`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances, we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: zero of nine conditions reaches $p < 0.05$, while multiple conditions show significant degradation.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	More than twenty remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling.
8	The bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale and universal across architectures.
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Self-Consistency and extensions.}
5	SC selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions include universal SC for free-form generation \citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \citep{li2023diverse}, and reasoning-aware filtering \citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. \citet{loo2025reevaluating} show that SC gains plateau early. Our Condorcet analysis explains this: $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ compresses $K_{\text{eff}}$ to 1.20--3.62 for $K = 12$ (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}
8	Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \citep{olausson2023linc} translate problems into FOL for external provers; LINC documents ${\sim}$40\% translation errors. More recent systems extend this: DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos} selects solver types, Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition, FoVer \citep{wang2025fover} trains FOL verifiers, and LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent} employs multi-perspective grounding. Each introduces capabilities beyond the reasoning model. \citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show that LLM formalization degrades with complexity, consistent with our findings. Our oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter); the failure localizes to self-extraction quality (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Test-time compute and verification.}
11	Scaling test-time computation through additional traces \citep{snell2025scaling} faces the same correlation challenge: same-model attempts collapse into correlated solutions. Reasoning models intensify this (QwQ-32B: $C_0 = 2.6\%$). Trained verifiers offer an alternative: outcome reward models \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}, process reward models \citep{lightman2024prm}, and Math-Shepherd \citep{wang2024mathshepherd}. Multi-agent debate refines answers through discussion \citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}. Self-correction without external feedback degrades performance \citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification}, paralleling our symbolic confirmation bias finding (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}): the same model that produces an error cannot reliably generate constraints that correct it.
12	
13	\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}
14	CJT \citep{condorcet1785} establishes that independent majority voting converges to truth. \citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size. \citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM ensemble classification. We extend this to reasoning-trace aggregation, measuring $\kappa$ and $K_{\text{eff}}$ across 15 conditions (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	\label{sec:exp-setup}
6	
7	We evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances. Beyond accuracy comparisons, we conduct diagnostic analyses of symbolic confirmation bias and process-level trace independence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Models.} Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-14B (non-thinking mode)~\citep{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\citep{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{dubey2024llama3}, and QwQ-32B~\citep{qwen2024qwq} (7B--32B parameters).
10	
11	\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\citep{han2022folio} ($n{=}204$, with gold FOL annotations), ProofWriter depth-5~\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter} ($n{=}600$), LogiQA~\citep{liu2020logiqa} ($n{=}200$). StrategyQA~\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. GSM8K~\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n{=}30$) and AIME 2024 ($n{=}22$) probe the degenerate regime ($C_0 < 5\%$).
12	
13	\paragraph{Parameters.} $K{=}12$ traces at $T{=}0.7$; extraction at $T_{\text{ext}}{=}0.3$. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\alpha{=}0.5$. Statistical significance via McNemar exact test ($\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\% bootstrap CIs from 10,000 resamples for $n \geq 200$.
14	
15	
16	\subsection{Main Results}
17	\label{sec:main-results}
18	
19	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports accuracy across all conditions sorted by SC baseline. SICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime.
20	
21	\begin{table*}[t]
22	\centering
23	\small
24	\caption{SICA vs.\ SC across all conditions sorted by SC accuracy. $\Delta$ = SICA $-$ SC (pp). $^b$In-context learning with gold FOL examples. $\dagger$\,$n < 100$ (suggestive only). \textbf{Bold $\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$ (McNemar). {*}\,$p < 0.05$;\enspace{**}\,$p < 0.01$;\enspace{***}\,$p < 0.001$. Failure modes detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}.}
25	\label{tab:main_results}
26	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}
27	\toprule
28	Model & Domain & $n$ & SC (\%) & SICA (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\
29	\midrule
30	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\leq$ 40\%)}} \\
31	Mistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\
32	\midrule
33	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\sim$50--55\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\
34	Mistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 51.00 & 51.50 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\
35	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\
36	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s42) & 204 & 55.88 & \textbf{59.31} & $+$3.43 & 0.092 \\
37	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\
38	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 54.41 & 53.92 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\
39	\midrule
40	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\geq$ 60\%)}} \\
41	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\
42	LLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\
43	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\
44	Qwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 75.17 & 72.67 & $\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\
45	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & 74.51 & $-$0.98 & 1.000 \\
46	Qwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\
47	Qwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 78.50 & 78.00 & $-$0.50 & 1.000 \\
48	Qwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 84.80 & \textbf{84.31} & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\
49	Qwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.00 & 85.67 & $+$0.67 & 1.000 \\
50	\midrule
51	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Degenerate ($C_0 < 5\%$)}} \\
52	QwQ-32B & AIME 2024$^\dagger$ & 22 & 72.70 & 72.70 & 0.00 & \textrm{---} \\
53	Qwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\dagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & \textrm{---} \\
54	\midrule
55	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL/DSL + Z3)}} \\
56	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & \textbf{89.71} & $\mathbf{+14.22}$ & $2.0{\times}10^{-7}${***} \\
57	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & \textbf{86.67} & $\mathbf{+11.50}$ & $9.21{\times}10^{-15}${***} \\
58	\bottomrule
59	\end{tabular}
60	\end{table*}
61	
62	\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\sim$50--55\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\,pp. Mistral-7B on LogiQA, despite the same SC range, yields only $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$), attributable to domain formalizability (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}).
63	
64	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ 60\%): confirmation bias.} Nine conditions with SC $\geq 63\%$ (combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.
65	
66	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.
67	
68	\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
69	
70	Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter} visualizes the regime dependence: the intermediate-SC region is narrow and observed only for Mistral-7B on FOLIO.
71	
72	\begin{figure}[t]
73	\centering
74	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}
75	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--55\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}
76	\label{fig:regime-scatter}
77	\end{figure}
78	
79	
80	\subsection{Oracle Control}
81	\label{sec:oracle}
82	
83	Oracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA achieves 89.71\% on FOLIO ($\Delta = +14.22$\,pp, $p = 2.0 \times 10^{-7}$) and 86.67\% on ProofWriter ($\Delta = +11.50$\,pp, $p = 9.21 \times 10^{-15}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\% (ProofWriter) of problems.
84	
85	These results establish that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given discriminative constraints. Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\,pp) captures only 24.1\% of the FOLIO oracle ceiling. The remaining 75.9\% is lost to self-extraction quality.
86	
87	
88	\subsection{Four-Seed Replication}
89	\label{sec:replication}
90	
91	Four seeds on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$, $K = 12$, $T = 0.7$) yield $\Delta$ from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp (mean $+1.47$\,pp); 2 of 4 show marginal trends ($p < 0.10$), none reaches $p < 0.05$. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), indicating seed-level constraint quality---not SC level alone---drives the effect (entropy decomposition in Appendix~\ref{app:entropy}).
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:exp-setup}\n\nWe evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances. Beyond accuracy comparisons, we conduct diagnostic analyses of symbolic confirmation bias and process-level trace independence (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Models.} Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-14B (non-thinking mode)~\\citep{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\\citep{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{dubey2024llama3}, and QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwen2024qwq} (7B--32B parameters).\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\\citep{han2022folio} ($n{=}204$, with gold FOL annotations), ProofWriter depth-5~\\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter} ($n{=}600$), LogiQA~\\citep{liu2020logiqa} ($n{=}200$). StrategyQA~\\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. GSM8K~\\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n{=}30$) and AIME 2024 ($n{=}22$) probe the degenerate regime ($C_0 < 5\\%$).\n\n\\paragraph{Parameters.} $K{=}12$ traces at $T{=}0.7$; extraction at $T_{\\text{ext}}{=}0.3$. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\\alpha{=}0.5$. Statistical significance via McNemar exact test ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\\% bootstrap CIs from 10,000 resamples for $n \\geq 200$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports accuracy across all conditions sorted by SC baseline. SICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SICA vs.\\ SC across all conditions sorted by SC accuracy. $\\Delta$ = SICA $-$ SC (pp). $^b$In-context learning with gold FOL examples. $\\dagger$\\,$n < 100$ (suggestive only). \\textbf{Bold $\\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$ (McNemar). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$;\\enspace{**}\\,$p < 0.01$;\\enspace{***}\\,$p < 0.001$. Failure modes detailed in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $n$ & SC (\\%) & SICA (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s42) & 204 & 55.88 & \\textbf{59.31} & $+$3.43 & 0.092 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 54.41 & 53.92 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%)}} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 75.17 & 72.67 & $\\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & 74.51 & $-$0.98 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 78.50 & 78.00 & $-$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 84.80 & \\textbf{84.31} & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.00 & 85.67 & $+$0.67 & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL/DSL + Z3)}} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & \\textbf{89.71} & $\\mathbf{+14.22}$ & $2.0{\\times}10^{-7}${***} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & \\textbf{86.67} & $\\mathbf{+11.50}$ & $9.21{\\times}10^{-15}${***} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\\sim$50--55\\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\\,pp. Mistral-7B on LogiQA, despite the same SC range, yields only $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$), attributable to domain formalizability (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%): confirmation bias.} Nine conditions with SC $\\geq 63\\%$ (combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Type C (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\\%$) shows $\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \\emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime-scatter} visualizes the regime dependence: the intermediate-SC region is narrow and observed only for Mistral-7B on FOLIO.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}\n\\caption{SICA $\\Delta$ (pp) vs.\\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \\geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--55\\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}\n\\label{fig:regime-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nOracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA achieves 89.71\\% on FOLIO ($\\Delta = +14.22$\\,pp, $p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$) and 86.67\\% on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = +11.50$\\,pp, $p = 9.21 \\times 10^{-15}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems.\n\nThese results establish that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given discriminative constraints. Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 24.1\\% of the FOLIO oracle ceiling. The remaining 75.9\\% is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Four-Seed Replication}\n\\label{sec:replication}\n\nFour seeds on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$, $K = 12$, $T = 0.7$) yield $\\Delta$ from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp (mean $+1.47$\\,pp); 2 of 4 show marginal trends ($p < 0.10$), none reaches $p < 0.05$. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), indicating seed-level constraint quality---not SC level alone---drives the effect (entropy decomposition in Appendix~\\ref{app:entropy}).",

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}
5	\label{sec:condorcet}
6	
7	Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \to \infty$ \citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:
8	\begin{equation}
9	\label{eq:effk}
10	K_{\text{eff}} = \frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \cdot \kappa}.
11	\end{equation}
12	In our experiments (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.2\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. This \emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.
13	
14	\subsection{SICA Pipeline}
15	\label{sec:pipeline}
16	
17	Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
18	
19	\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\{t_1, \ldots, t_K\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.
20	
21	\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}). The \emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.
22	
23	\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:
24	\begin{equation}
25	\label{eq:maxsat}
26	\mathcal{C}^* = \arg\max_{\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{C}} \sum_{c \in \mathcal{S}} w_c \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \mathcal{S} \text{ is satisfiable.}
27	\end{equation}
28	Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\texttt{answer\_a} == \texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.
29	
30	\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus} = \mathcal{C} \setminus \mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:
31	\begin{equation}
32	\label{eq:score}
33	\text{score}(a) = \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^*} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a) - \alpha \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a),
34	\end{equation}
35	where $\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \arg\max_{a} \text{score}(a)$.
36	
37	\paragraph{Degeneration under confirmation bias.} Self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answer. While ${\sim}$92\% are technically discriminative (Appendix~\ref{app:extraction}), their direction mirrors the model's answer distribution. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:degenerate}
40	\text{score}(a) \approx \sum_{\substack{c \in \mathcal{C}^* \\ \text{source}(c) \to a}} w_c,
41	\end{equation}
42	equivalent to majority voting. The ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically.
43	
44	\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ (fraction of questions with $>1$ distinct answer) is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.
45	\begin{equation}
46	\label{eq:c0-def}
47	C_0 = \frac{|\{q \in \mathcal{Q} : |\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\}|}{|\mathcal{Q}|}.
48	\end{equation}
49	
50	\begin{figure}[t]
51	\centering
52	\small
53	\begin{tikzpicture}[
54	    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,
55	    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,
56	                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\scriptsize},
57	    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},
58	    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},
59	    lbl/.style={font=\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},
60	    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,
61	                    inner sep=3pt},
62	]
63	\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};
64	\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\traces};
65	\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\constraints};
66	\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\ +\\weight};
67	\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\(Z3)};
68	
69	\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};
70	\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\vote};
71	
72	\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);
73	\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);
74	\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);
75	\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);
76	\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);
77	\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);
78	
79	\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};
80	
81	\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\scriptsize\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};
82	
83	\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};
84	\end{tikzpicture}
85	\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}
86	\label{fig:pipeline}
87	\end{figure}
88	
89	\subsection{Oracle Control Design}
90	\label{sec:oracle-design}
91	
92	To isolate whether the bottleneck lies in extraction or aggregation, we introduce an oracle control, following the compositionality-gap methodology of \citet{press2023compositionality}. Gold FOL annotations (FOLIO) or DSL rules (ProofWriter) replace self-extracted constraints; Z3 evaluates them against each candidate answer, with scoring unchanged (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}). If the oracle produces large gains while self-extraction does not, the failure localizes to extraction quality. Results appear in \S\ref{sec:oracle}.
93	
94	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
95	\label{sec:process-kappa-method}
96	
97	Answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ may underestimate the true independence bottleneck. Two traces that follow nearly identical reasoning can reach different final answers; $\kappa$ treats such pairs as disagreeing, overestimating independence. We quantify process-level similarity via pairwise cosine similarity over TF-IDF representations of chain-of-thought traces. For problem $q$ with $K$ traces:
98	\begin{equation}
99	\label{eq:process-sim}
100	\bar{s}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i, \mathbf{v}_j),
101	\end{equation}
102	where $\mathbf{v}_i$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$. When $\bar{s} \gg \kappa$, reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answers suggest. This metric applies to any trace aggregation method, not only SICA.
103	
104	\subsection{Scope}
105	\label{sec:scope}
106	
107	SICA uses \emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM generates traces and extracts constraints. We test more than twenty remediation strategies: nine prompt-level spanning six dimensions, three method-level (ICG, Contrastive, Debate), and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck from different angles (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). Methods using trained extractors, external provers, or multi-model ensembles fall outside scope.
```

Gold FOL annotations (FOLIO) or DSL rules (ProofWriter) replace self-extracted constraints; Z3 evaluates them against each candidate answer, with scoring unchanged (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}). If the oracle produces large gains while self-extraction does not, the failure localizes to extraction quality. Results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:oracle}.\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa-method}\n\nAnswer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ may underestimate the true independence bottleneck. Two traces that follow nearly identical reasoning can reach different final answers; $\\kappa$ treats such pairs as disagreeing, overestimating independence. We quantify process-level similarity via pairwise cosine similarity over TF-IDF representations of chain-of-thought traces. For problem $q$ with $K$ traces:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\bar{s}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i, \\mathbf{v}_j),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$. When $\\bar{s} \\gg \\kappa$, reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answers suggest. This metric applies to any trace aggregation method, not only SICA.\n\n\\subsection{Scope}\n\\label{sec:scope}\n\nSICA uses \\emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM generates traces and extracts constraints. We test more than twenty remediation strategies: nine prompt-level spanning six dimensions, three method-level (ICG, Contrastive, Debate), and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck from different angles (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). Methods using trained extractors, external provers, or multi-model ensembles fall outside scope.",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 107
    }

```text
1	Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\S\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The intermediate-SC region where positive trends occur is narrow (SC $\approx$ 50--55\%) and observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. All models range from 7B to 32B; whether the bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) remains untested. The oracle analysis covers two formal logic benchmarks with gold annotations (FOLIO, ProofWriter); extending to commonsense or open-domain reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets. Four-seed replication shows effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp, with 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}). All evaluation datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces (binary or multiple-choice); whether confirmation bias generalizes to open-ended generation or multilingual settings is untested. The process-level independence metric (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}) uses TF-IDF representations, which may underweight semantic similarity not captured by lexical overlap. Additional limitations regarding power analysis, prompt templates, and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations-extended}.
```

  "text": "[写作审查请求] Step 5 Round 1/5\n\nWriting plan: projects/symb_invariant_consensus/writing_plan.yaml\nTarget venue: EMNLP 2026 ARR (8pp main text, unlimited references/appendix; Limitations exempt)\nCurrent state: 21 pages total (main text ~8.3pp, slightly over limit). 0 critical compilation warnings.\n\n## 全文 LaTeX\n\n### abstract.tex\nSelf-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances, we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: zero of nine conditions reaches $p < 0.05$, while multiple conditions show significant degradation.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nMore than twenty remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling.\nThe bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale and universal across architectures.\n\n### introduction.tex\n§1 Introduction: Motivates SC plateau via CJT independence violation. Introduces SICA pipeline (K traces → FOL extraction → dedup → MAX-SAT via Z3). States 4 contributions: (C1) Independence-Accuracy Trade-off with Pareto frontier across 4 methods and 3 families; (C2) Symbolic Confirmation Bias — bias ratio 4.97×, 94% error overlap; (C3) Process-Level Independence — CoT cosine 0.69 >> κ 0.41; (C4) Regime-Dependent Pattern with C0 diagnostic. Key numbers: 6 models, 3 domains, 3000+ instances, 4-seed replication, 20+ remediation strategies all fail.\n\n### related_work.tex\n§2 Related Work: 4 paragraphs — SC extensions (USC, CISC, diverse prompting, reevaluating SC plateau), neuro-symbolic reasoning (Logic-LM, LINC, DyLoS, Aristotle, FoVer, LogicAgent + oracle control connection), test-time compute and verification (scaling, trained verifiers, debate, self-correction parallel), CJT and ensemble diversity (Condorcet, correlated voters, LLM ensemble classification).\n\n### method.tex\n§3 Method: (3.1) SC + Condorcet with Eff-K formula via Fleiss' κ; (3.2) SICA Pipeline — trace gen, constraint extraction, dedup + MAX-SAT (Eq 2), scoring (Eq 3), degeneration analysis (Eq 4), C0 diagnostic (Eq 5), TikZ pipeline figure; (3.3) Oracle Control Design; (3.4) Process-Level Independence — TF-IDF cosine metric (Eq 6); (3.5) Scope statement.\n\n### experiments.tex\n§4 Experiments: (4.1) Setup — 6 models, 3+3 datasets, K=12, T=0.7, McNemar test; (4.2) Main Results — Table 1 (18 conditions), 4 regimes: Intermediate-SC (Mistral FOLIO +1.47pp mean, no p<0.05), Type A confirmation bias (9 conditions, no positive), Type C error amplification (Mistral PW -2.83pp p=0.014), Degenerate (C0<5%); Figure 2 regime scatter; (4.3) Oracle Control — +14.22pp FOLIO, +11.50pp PW, self-extraction captures 24%; (4.4) Four-Seed Replication — Δ from +3.43 to -0.49, seed-level quality drives variation.\n\n### analysis.tex\n§5 Analysis: (5.1) Condorcet Effective-K — Table 2 (15 conditions), κ 0.21-0.91, Eff-K 1.09-3.62; cross-model κ validation (Table 4); (5.2) Symbolic Confirmation Bias — 83.3% wrong constraints on errors, bias ratio 4.97×, 94% overlap, CEGIS oscillation, cross-model extraction ±1pp; (5.3) Process-Level Independence — cosine 0.69 >> κ 0.41, r=-0.06, 9.3% convergent pairs, Figure 3 scatter; (5.4) Supporting Diagnostics — extraction taxonomy, constraint ablation, hyperparameter sensitivity, entropy decomposition; (5.5) Remediation Analysis — 9 prompt + 3 method (Table 3) + 10 additional (Appendix Table 6, Figure 5), independence-accuracy trade-off (Table 4), Pareto frontier (Figure 4).\n\n### discussion.tex\n§6 Discussion: 4 paragraphs — symbolic confirmation bias as unifying root cause (direct + indirect channels), process-level implications (gap between 0.69 and 0.41), practitioner diagnostics (C0 + SC thresholds), scope and paths forward (scaling κ, published systems bypass self-extraction).\n\n### conclusion.tex\n§7 Conclusion: Single paragraph summarizing key findings — 6 models, 3 domains, 3000+ instances, confirmation bias 4.97×/94% overlap, process-level 0.69>>0.41, 20+ strategies fail, Pareto κ≥0.40, oracle +14.22/+11.50pp, 24% ceiling, C0 diagnostic.\n\n### limitations_content.tex\nCovers: no trained extractors/external solvers tested, narrow intermediate-SC region, 7B-32B scale only, English logical reasoning only, TF-IDF limitations, 4-seed variance, optimal K/T need labeled data.\n\n## 审查重点\n\n1. **页数限制**：主文本约 8.3 页，略超 EMNLP 8 页限制，需要压缩建议\n2. **R-independent sections 审查**：§2 Related Work, §3 Method, §4.1-4.3 Experiments, §6 Discussion — 这些章节的数据完整，请重点审查\n3. **叙事一致性**：4 个 contributions 是否在各章节一一对应\n4. **数字一致性**：Abstract/Intro/Conclusion/Tables 中的关键数字是否一致\n\n## 额外审查维度\n\n除标准的 Originality/Clarity/Soundness/Significance/Presentation 维度外，请额外检查：\n- 叙事一致性（各章节是否讲同一个故事）\n- 贡献-范围对齐（Intro contribution ↔ 正文 section 1:1）\n- 去 AI 风格（em-dash、-ing、filler words）\n- 页面预算分配合理性\n参考 reviewer-audit-checklist §7。",
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus"
}

1	
2	\section{Prompt Templates}
3	\label{app:prompts}
4	
5	SICA uses domain-specific prompt templates for constraint extraction: one for mathematical reasoning (GSM8K, AIME) and one for logical reasoning (ProofWriter, FOLIO). Both share the same system prompt. The LLM is called with temperature $T{=}0.3$, max tokens $1024$, and structured JSON output mode. Up to 6 traces are processed in parallel via thread pooling, with 2 retries on failure (exponential backoff, base 0.5s).
6	
7	\paragraph{System Prompt.}
8	
9	\begin{lstlisting}
10	You are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY
11	valid JSON. Do NOT solve problems, reason about
12	correctness, or think step-by-step. Extract
13	constraints from the given trace exactly as stated.
14	\end{lstlisting}
15	
16	\paragraph{Mathematical Constraint Extraction (EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
17	
18	Used for GSM8K and AIME datasets. The placeholder \texttt{\{trace\}} is replaced with the reasoning trace at runtime.
19	
20	\begin{lstlisting}
21	Extract mathematical constraints from this reasoning
22	trace as a JSON object.
23	
24	Rules:
25	- type: "equation", "inequality", or "predicate"
26	- expression: human-readable, e.g. "x + y == 14"
27	- z3_formula: a single comparison expression in
28	  Python Z3 syntax
29	- source_step: step number where the constraint
30	  appears
31	- Use variable names from the trace
32	
33	z3_formula MUST follow these rules:
34	- Allowed operators: ==, !=, <, >, <=, >=, +, -, *,
35	  /, **, %
36	- Use individual named variables: a1, a2, a3...
37	  NOT arrays a[1] or sets A
38	- Expand all sums explicitly:
39	  "a1 + a2 + a3 == 55" NOT "sum([a1,a2,a3])"
40	- NO Python builtins: sum(), len(), max(), min(),
41	  list(), set(), range(), int()
42	- Each z3_formula must be exactly ONE comparison
43	  (==, !=, <, >, <=, >=)
44	- For multiple values, write separate constraints
45	  for each
46	
47	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
48	{"constraints": [{"type": "equation",
49	  "expression": "x + y == 14",
50	  "z3_formula": "x + y == 14",
51	  "source_step": 1}],
52	 "answer": "final_answer",
53	 "variables": ["x", "y"]}
54	
55	Trace:
56	{trace}
57	\end{lstlisting}
58	
59	\paragraph{Logical Constraint Extraction (LOGIC\_EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
60	
61	Used for ProofWriter and FOLIO datasets.
62	
63	\begin{lstlisting}
64	IMPORTANT: You are an EXTRACTION tool, NOT a problem
65	solver.
66	- Do NOT re-solve or re-reason about the problem.
67	- Do NOT verify whether the trace's reasoning is
68	  correct.
69	- ONLY extract the logical constraints that are
70	  explicitly stated or used in the trace below.
71	- Output ONLY the JSON object, nothing else.
72	
73	Extract logical constraints from this reasoning trace
74	about a logic problem.
75	
76	The problem has facts (base assertions), rules
77	(grounded implications), and derived conclusions.
78	Convert each into a Z3 boolean formula.
79	
80	Variable naming:
81	- Unary property: property_entity
82	  (e.g., kind_anne, red_charlie, round_fiona)
83	- Binary relation: relation_subject_object
84	  (e.g., eats_bear_squirrel, likes_lion_cat)
85	- Use lowercase with underscores only
86	
87	Constraint types:
88	- "fact": a given assertion,
89	  e.g., kind_anne == True
90	- "rule": a grounded implication over specific
91	  entities, e.g., Implies(kind_anne, nice_anne)
92	- "derived": an inferred conclusion from facts +
93	  rules, e.g., nice_anne == True
94	
95	Z3 operators:
96	- Implies(a, b) for if-then rules
97	- And(a, b, ...) for conjunctive conditions
98	- Or(a, b, ...) for disjunctive conditions
99	- Not(a) for negation
100	- == True / == False for boolean assertions

128	  ],
129	  "answer": "&lt;final answer string>",
130	  "variables": ["&lt;var1>", "&lt;var2>", "..."]
131	}
132	\end{lstlisting}
133	
134	The pipeline additionally injects an \texttt{answer\_assertion} constraint per trace (not produced by the LLM):
135	
136	\begin{lstlisting}
137	{"type": "answer_assertion",
138	 "expression": "answer is &lt;ans>",
139	 "z3_formula": "__sica_answer_&lt;ans> == True",
140	 "source_step": -1}
141	\end{lstlisting}
142	
143	\paragraph{Prompt Selection Logic.}
144	
145	Domain assignment is determined by dataset: FOLIO and ProofWriter use \textsc{Logic\_Extraction\_Prompt}; GSM8K, AIME, StrategyQA, and ARC-Challenge use \textsc{Extraction\_Prompt}. StrategyQA and ARC-Challenge are assigned to the mathematical prompt because their reasoning structure does not involve formal logic predicates or implications, despite StrategyQA producing boolean answers.
146	
147	
148	\section{Z3 MaxSAT Evaluation Pseudocode}
149	\label{app:z3-pseudocode}
150	
151	Algorithm~\ref{alg:maxsat} describes the Z3 MaxSAT solving procedure. Algorithm~\ref{alg:scorer} describes the source-trace attribution scoring.
152	
153	Domain constraint variables (e.g., \texttt{x + y == 14}) and answer assertion variables (e.g., \texttt{\_\_sica\_answer\_42 == True}) are logically independent in the Z3 formulation. Discriminative signal arises solely from conflicts among domain constraints: when traces supporting different answers produce mutually contradictory constraints, the solver excludes the lower-weight subset, and the scorer penalizes the corresponding answers.
154	
155	\begin{algorithm}[h]
156	\caption{Z3 Weighted Partial MAX-SAT}
157	\label{alg:maxsat}
158	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
159	\Require Deduplicated constraint set $\mathcal{C} = \{(c_m, w_m, S_m)\}_{m=1}^M$ where $c_m$ is a Z3 formula, $w_m$ is weight, $S_m$ is the set of source trace indices
160	\Ensure Partition $(\mathcal{C}^*, \mathcal{C}^{\setminus})$ where $\mathcal{C}^*$ is the max-weight satisfiable subset
161	\State $\textit{opt} \gets \textsc{Z3.Optimize()}$
162	\For{$m = 1, \ldots, M$}
163	    \State $b_m \gets$ fresh Boolean indicator
164	    \State $\textit{opt}.\textsc{Add}(b_m \Rightarrow c_m)$ \Comment{soft clause guarded by indicator}
165	    \State $\textit{opt}.\textsc{AddSoft}(b_m,\; \text{weight}=w_m)$
166	\EndFor
167	\State $\textit{result} \gets \textit{opt}.\textsc{Check}()$
168	\State $\textit{model} \gets \textit{opt}.\textsc{Model}()$
169	\State $\mathcal{C}^* \gets \{(c_m, w_m, S_m) : \textit{model}(b_m) = \top\}$
170	\State $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus} \gets \mathcal{C} \setminus \mathcal{C}^*$
171	\State \Return $(\mathcal{C}^*, \mathcal{C}^{\setminus})$
172	\end{algorithmic}
173	\end{algorithm}
174	
175	\begin{algorithm}[h]
176	\caption{Source-Trace Attribution Scoring}
177	\label{alg:scorer}
178	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
179	\Require Satisfied set $\mathcal{C}^*$, excluded set $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}$, candidate answers $\mathcal{A}$, trace-answer mapping $\tau: \text{trace\_idx} \to a$, penalty weight $\alpha$
180	\Ensure Score $s(a)$ for each $a \in \mathcal{A}$
181	\For{each $a \in \mathcal{A}$}
182	    \State $T_a \gets \{i : \tau(i) = a\}$ \Comment{trace indices producing answer $a$}
183	    \State $\textit{pos}(a) \gets \sum_{(c,w,S) \in \mathcal{C}^*} w \cdot \mathbf{1}[S \cap T_a \neq \emptyset]$
184	    \State $\textit{neg}(a) \gets \sum_{(c,w,S) \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w \cdot \mathbf{1}[S \cap T_a \neq \emptyset]$
185	    \State $s(a) \gets \textit{pos}(a) - \alpha \cdot \textit{neg}(a)$
186	\EndFor
187	\State \Return $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} s(a)$
188	\end{algorithmic}
189	\end{algorithm}
190	
191	
192	\section{Capability Trap Diagnostics}
193	\label{app:diagnostics}
194	
195	Table~\ref{tab:capability_trap} reports per-condition diagnostic metrics referenced in \S\ref{sec:entropy}.
196	
197	\begin{table}[h]
198	\centering
199	\small
```

222	\section{Statistical Power Analysis}
223	\label{app:power}
224	
225	We compute the minimum detectable effect (MDE) for each condition at $\alpha = 0.05$ and 80\% power using exact binomial power calculations over McNemar discordant pairs. Table~\ref{tab:power} reports three grouped conditions with $n \geq 200$ (Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO and ProofWriter are pooled into a single $n{=}804$ analysis, corresponding to four Table~\ref{tab:main_results} rows), which constitute our confirmatory analyses. The remaining seven conditions ($n \leq 60$) yield infinite MDE due to insufficient discordant pairs and serve only as regime characterization.
226	
227	\begin{table}[h]
228	\centering
229	\small
230	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
231	\toprule
232	\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \textbf{MDE} & \textbf{Obs.\ $\Delta$} & \textbf{Obs.\ Pow.} \\
233	\midrule
234	Qwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & 1.86pp & $+$0.25pp & 5.7\%  \\
235	LLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & 4.64pp & $+$2.00pp & 20.6\% \\
236	LLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & 4.84pp & $-$4.41pp & 18.6\% \\
237	\bottomrule
238	\end{tabular}
239	\caption{Power analysis for confirmatory conditions ($n \geq 200$). MDE = minimum detectable effect at 80\% power. All observed effects fall below their respective MDEs, and observed power remains below 21\% in every case.}
240	\label{tab:power}
241	\end{table}
242	
243	The largest study (Qwen-14B, $n{=}804$, 30 discordant pairs) can detect effects as small as 1.86pp, yet the observed $\Delta$ of $+$0.25pp is an order of magnitude smaller. The two LLaMA-8B conditions require effects of 4.64pp and 4.84pp to reach significance; neither observed $\Delta$ approaches these thresholds. All three conditions are consistent with the null hypothesis that SICA provides no accuracy gain over self-consistency.
244

267	All three intervals contain zero. The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.
268	
269	
270	\section{Case Studies: Discordant Predictions}
271	\label{app:case-studies}
272	
273	Among 804 problems, SICA and SC agree on 773 (96.1\%). The 31 discordant cases comprise 16 SICA wins (SICA correct, SC wrong), 14 SC wins (SC correct, SICA wrong), and 1 where both select different wrong answers. All 31 occur at exact ties (entropy${=}1.0$, 6:6 vote split) or near-ties (entropy${\geq}0.9183$, 8:4 at most), since SICA can differ from SC only when constraint weights shift the majority away from SC's tiebreak choice. We present three representative cases that illustrate the mechanism behind capability trap failures.
274	
275	\paragraph{Case 1: SICA Wins (folio\_134, FOLIO).}
276	
277	\emph{Premises}: Some mammals have teeth. Platypus have no teeth. Platypus are mammals. Humans have teeth. \emph{Conclusion}: Humans are mammals. \emph{Gold}: Unknown.
278	
279	The premises state that \emph{some} mammals have teeth and that humans have teeth, but ``having teeth'' is not sufficient for ``being a mammal'' under open-world semantics. SC produces a 6:6 split (Unknown:6, True:6) and tiebreaks alphabetically to True (wrong). SICA extracts 7 constraints; MaxSAT excludes 1 constraint inconsistent with the rest, reducing the weighted score for True-voting traces and tipping the aggregation to Unknown (correct). The excluded constraint likely captured an incorrect inference step (e.g., affirming the consequent) present only in True-voting traces. While SICA succeeds here, the success is coincidental: the excluded constraint happens to anti-correlate with the correct answer.
280	
281	\paragraph{Case 2: SC Wins (ProofWriter-1066\_Q6, ProofWriter).}
282	
283	\emph{Key chain}: White(Bob)$\to$Round(Bob) [white things are round] $\to$ Young(Bob) [all round things are young]. \emph{Conclusion}: ``Bob is not young.'' \emph{Gold}: False (Bob IS young).
284	
285	SC produces a 6:6 split (False:6, True:6) and tiebreaks to False (correct). SICA extracts 17 constraints; MaxSAT excludes 2 that appear preferentially in False-voting traces. These excluded constraints likely captured correct intermediate derivations (e.g., \texttt{young\_bob == True}) that appeared only in traces completing the full deduction chain. By excluding them, MaxSAT removes evidence supporting the correct answer. SICA selects True (wrong). This case illustrates how self-extracted constraints can systematically mislead: correct reasoning steps become discriminative signals that the solver mistakenly penalizes.
286	
287	\paragraph{Case 3: Non-discriminative Constraints (ProofWriter-903\_Q6, ProofWriter).}
288	
289	\emph{Key chain}: Nice(Mouse)$\to$Cold(Mouse) [all nice people are cold] $\to$ Chases(Mouse,Mouse) [nice and cold $\to$ chase mouse]. \emph{Conclusion}: ``The mouse does not chase the mouse.'' \emph{Gold}: False.
290	
291	SC produces a 6:6 split (False:6, Unknown:6) and tiebreaks to False (correct). SICA extracts 44 constraints but excludes only 1 (2.3\%). The remaining 43 are domain facts and grounded rules (\texttt{nice\_mouse == True}, \texttt{Implies(nice\_mouse, cold\_mouse)}, etc.) that Z3 satisfies identically regardless of the final answer. Both False-voting and Unknown-voting traces share the same premises and grounded rules; they differ only in whether the trace completed the full derivation chain. The single excluded constraint carries negligible weight against 43 shared constraints, so SICA collapses to SC.
292	
293	\paragraph{Pattern.}
294	
295	These cases exemplify the general pattern across the 773 agreeing problems: the vast majority of extracted constraints encode shared domain structure rather than answer-specific reasoning, and MaxSAT aggregation degenerates to majority vote. Table~\ref{tab:case_summary} summarizes.
296

298	\centering
299	\small
300	\caption{Summary of three representative discordant cases. All occur at exact 6:6 vote ties. Constraint exclusion can break ties in either direction (Cases 1--2) or have no effect (Case 3).}
301	\label{tab:case_summary}

303	\toprule
304	Case & ID & Gold & SC & SICA & \#Constr. & Excl.\ (\%) \\
305	\midrule
306	1 (SICA win) & folio\_134 & Unknown & True $\times$ & Unknown $\checkmark$ & 7 & 1 (14.3\%) \\
307	2 (SC win) & PW-1066\_Q6 & False & False $\checkmark$ & True $\times$ & 17 & 2 (11.8\%) \\
308	3 (Non-disc.) & PW-903\_Q6 & False & False $\checkmark$ & False $\checkmark$ & 44 & 1 (2.3\%) \\

313	
314	\section{ProofWriter Oracle Per-Subtype Analysis}
315	\label{app:pw-oracle-subtype}
316	
317	ProofWriter problems encode deductive rules as Domain-Specific Language (DSL) logic programs. Each problem uses one of four predicate subtypes: attribute predicates with or without negation (AttNeg, AttNoneg), and relation predicates with or without negation (RelNeg, RelNoneg). Relation predicates involve binary relations between entities (e.g., \texttt{eats(bear, squirrel)}), while attribute predicates assign unary properties (e.g., \texttt{kind(anne)}).
318	
319	The oracle pipeline parses these DSL annotations deterministically into Z3 formulas without any LLM involvement. Of the 600 ProofWriter problems, 594 (99.0\%) produce valid Z3 parses; the remaining 6 fail due to unsupported DSL constructs. Across all 7{,}128 trace-problem pairs submitted to Z3, the solver returns a result in every case (0\% solver errors). Z3 evaluations agree with ground-truth labels on 577 of 594 parseable problems (97.14\%), confirming that the deterministic DSL-to-Z3 translation faithfully preserves problem semantics.
320	
321	Table~\ref{tab:pw_oracle_subtype} reports the per-subtype breakdown. Relation-predicate subtypes show substantially larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes: RelNeg improves by $+17.86$~pp and RelNoneg by $+13.01$~pp, compared to $+9.09$~pp for AttNoneg and $+6.87$~pp for AttNeg. Relation predicates involve longer reasoning chains with more intermediate entities, producing greater answer diversity across traces ($C_0$) and more opportunities for oracle constraints to correct errors.
322	
323	\begin{table}[h]
324	\centering
325	\caption{ProofWriter oracle experiment per-subtype breakdown. Oracle-SICA uses gold DSL annotations parsed deterministically to Z3 for consistency checking. Relation-predicate subtypes (RelNeg, RelNoneg) show larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes; relation subtypes involve longer reasoning chains and benefit more from oracle constraints. Bold indicates best accuracy per row.}
326	\label{tab:pw_oracle_subtype}
327	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
328	\toprule
329	Subtype & $n$ & SC Acc (\%) & Oracle Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
330	\midrule
331	AttNoneg & 154 & 87.01 & \textbf{96.10} & +9.09 \\
332	AttNeg & 160 & 85.00 & \textbf{91.88} & +6.87 \\
333	RelNoneg & 146 & 66.44 & \textbf{79.45} & +13.01 \\
334	RelNeg & 140 & 60.00 & \textbf{77.86} & +17.86 \\
335	\midrule
336	Overall & 600 & 75.17 & \textbf{86.67} & +11.50 \\
337	\bottomrule

The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.\n\n\n\\section{Case Studies: Discordant Predictions}\n\\label{app:case-studies}\n\nAmong 804 problems, SICA and SC agree on 773 (96.1\\%). The 31 discordant cases comprise 16 SICA wins (SICA correct, SC wrong), 14 SC wins (SC correct, SICA wrong), and 1 where both select different wrong answers. All 31 occur at exact ties (entropy${=}1.0$, 6:6 vote split) or near-ties (entropy${\\geq}0.9183$, 8:4 at most), since SICA can differ from SC only when constraint weights shift the majority away from SC's tiebreak choice. We present three representative cases that illustrate the mechanism behind capability trap failures.\n\n\\paragraph{Case 1: SICA Wins (folio\\_134, FOLIO).}\n\n\\emph{Premises}: Some mammals have teeth. Platypus have no teeth. Platypus are mammals. Humans have teeth. \\emph{Conclusion}: Humans are mammals. \\emph{Gold}: Unknown.\n\nThe premises state that \\emph{some} mammals have teeth and that humans have teeth, but ``having teeth'' is not sufficient for ``being a mammal'' under open-world semantics. SC produces a 6:6 split (Unknown:6, True:6) and tiebreaks alphabetically to True (wrong). SICA extracts 7 constraints; MaxSAT excludes 1 constraint inconsistent with the rest, reducing the weighted score for True-voting traces and tipping the aggregation to Unknown (correct). The excluded constraint likely captured an incorrect inference step (e.g., affirming the consequent) present only in True-voting traces. While SICA succeeds here, the success is coincidental: the excluded constraint happens to anti-correlate with the correct answer.\n\n\\paragraph{Case 2: SC Wins (ProofWriter-1066\\_Q6, ProofWriter).}\n\n\\emph{Key chain}: White(Bob)$\\to$Round(Bob) [white things are round] $\\to$ Young(Bob) [all round things are young]. \\emph{Conclusion}: ``Bob is not young.'' \\emph{Gold}: False (Bob IS young).\n\nSC produces a 6:6 split (False:6, True:6) and tiebreaks to False (correct). SICA extracts 17 constraints; MaxSAT excludes 2 that appear preferentially in False-voting traces. These excluded constraints likely captured correct intermediate derivations (e.g., \\texttt{young\\_bob == True}) that appeared only in traces completing the full deduction chain. By excluding them, MaxSAT removes evidence supporting the correct answer. SICA selects True (wrong). This case illustrates how self-extracted constraints can systematically mislead: correct reasoning steps become discriminative signals that the solver mistakenly penalizes.\n\n\\paragraph{Case 3: Non-discriminative Constraints (ProofWriter-903\\_Q6, ProofWriter).}\n\n\\emph{Key chain}: Nice(Mouse)$\\to$Cold(Mouse) [all nice people are cold] $\\to$ Chases(Mouse,Mouse) [nice and cold $\\to$ chase mouse]. \\emph{Conclusion}: ``The mouse does not chase the mouse.'' \\emph{Gold}: False.\n\nSC produces a 6:6 split (False:6, Unknown:6) and tiebreaks to False (correct). SICA extracts 44 constraints but excludes only 1 (2.3\\%). The remaining 43 are domain facts and grounded rules (\\texttt{nice\\_mouse == True}, \\texttt{Implies(nice\\_mouse, cold\\_mouse)}, etc.) that Z3 satisfies identically regardless of the final answer. Both False-voting and Unknown-voting traces share the same premises and grounded rules; they differ only in whether the trace completed the full derivation chain. The single excluded constraint carries negligible weight against 43 shared constraints, so SICA collapses to SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Pattern.}\n\nThese cases exemplify the general pattern across the 773 agreeing problems: the vast majority of extracted constraints encode shared domain structure rather than answer-specific reasoning, and MaxSAT aggregation degenerates to majority vote. Table~\\ref{tab:case_summary} summarizes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Summary of three representative discordant cases. All occur at exact 6:6 vote ties. Constraint exclusion can break ties in either direction (Cases 1--2) or have no effect (Case 3).}\n\\label{tab:case_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lllcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCase & ID & Gold & SC & SICA & \\#Constr. & Excl.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (SICA win) & folio\\_134 & Unknown & True $\\times$ & Unknown $\\checkmark$ & 7 & 1 (14.3\\%) \\\\\n2 (SC win) & PW-1066\\_Q6 & False & False $\\checkmark$ & True $\\times$ & 17 & 2 (11.8\\%) \\\\\n3 (Non-disc.) & PW-903\\_Q6 & False & False $\\checkmark$ & False $\\checkmark$ & 44 & 1 (2.3\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{ProofWriter Oracle Per-Subtype Analysis}\n\\label{app:pw-oracle-subtype}\n\nProofWriter problems encode deductive rules as Domain-Specific Language (DSL) logic programs. Each problem uses one of four predicate subtypes: attribute predicates with or without negation (AttNeg, AttNoneg), and relation predicates with or without negation (RelNeg, RelNoneg). Relation predicates involve binary relations between entities (e.g., \\texttt{eats(bear, squirrel)}), while attribute predicates assign unary properties (e.g., \\texttt{kind(anne)}).\n\nThe oracle pipeline parses these DSL annotations deterministically into Z3 formulas without any LLM involvement. Of the 600 ProofWriter problems, 594 (99.0\\%) produce valid Z3 parses; the remaining 6 fail due to unsupported DSL constructs. Across all 7{,}128 trace-problem pairs submitted to Z3, the solver returns a result in every case (0\\% solver errors). Z3 evaluations agree with ground-truth labels on 577 of 594 parseable problems (97.14\\%), confirming that the deterministic DSL-to-Z3 translation faithfully preserves problem semantics.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:pw_oracle_subtype} reports the per-subtype breakdown. Relation-predicate subtypes show substantially larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes: RelNeg improves by $+17.86$~pp and RelNoneg by $+13.01$~pp, compared to $+9.09$~pp for AttNoneg and $+6.87$~pp for AttNeg. Relation predicates involve longer reasoning chains with more intermediate entities, producing greater answer diversity across traces ($C_0$) and more opportunities for oracle constraints to correct errors.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{ProofWriter oracle experiment per-subtype breakdown. Oracle-SICA uses gold DSL annotations parsed deterministically to Z3 for consistency checking. Relation-predicate subtypes (RelNeg, RelNoneg) show larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes; relation subtypes involve longer reasoning chains and benefit more from oracle constraints. Bold indicates best accuracy per row.}\n\\label{tab:pw_oracle_subtype}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSubtype & $n$ & SC Acc (\\%) & Oracle Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nAttNoneg & 154 & 87.01 & \\textbf{96.10} & +9.09 \\\\\nAttNeg & 160 & 85.00 & \\textbf{91.88} & +6.87 \\\\\nRelNoneg & 146 & 66.44 & \\textbf{79.45} & +13.01 \\\\\nRelNeg & 140 & 60.00 & \\textbf{77.86} & +17.86 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOverall & 600 & 75.17 & \\textbf{86.67} & +11.50 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Constraint Ablation Details}\n\\label{app:ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Constraint ablation (Qwen2.5-14B, $n = 804$).
